Orbits of the left-right equivalence of maps in arbitrary characteristic Thanks: I was supported by the Israel Science Foundation, grants No. 1910/18 and 1405/22
Dmitry Kerner

Abstract
. The germs of maps ( 𝕜 n , o ) → f ( 𝕜 p , o ) (\Bbbk^{n},o)\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{\to}\limits^{f}}$}\ (\Bbbk^{p},o) are traditionally studied up to the right ( ℛ \mathscr{R} ), left-right ( 𝒜 \mathscr{A} ) or contact ( 𝒦 {\mathscr{K}}\! ) equivalence. Various questions about the group-orbits 𝒢 ​ f \mathscr{G}f (for 𝒢 \mathscr{G} one of ℛ , 𝒦 , 𝒜 \mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!,\mathscr{A} ) are reduced to their tangent spaces, T 𝒢 ​ f T_{\mathscr{G}}f . Classically the passage T 𝒢 ​ f ↝ 𝒢 ​ f T_{\mathscr{G}}f\rightsquigarrow\mathscr{G}f was done by vector fields integration, hence it was bound to the ℝ / ℂ \mathbb{R}/\mathbb{C} -analytic or C r C^{r} -category. The purely-algebraic (characteristic-free) approach to the group-orbits of ℛ , 𝒦 \mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\! has been developed during the last decades. But those methods could not address the (essentially more complicated) 𝒜 \mathscr{A} -equivalence. Moreover, the characteristic-free results (for ℛ , 𝒦 \mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\! ) were weaker than those in characteristic zero, because of the (inevitable) pathologies of positive characteristic. In this paper we close these omissions. • We establish the general (characteristic-free) passage T 𝒢 ​ f ↝ 𝒢 ​ f T_{\mathscr{G}}f\rightsquigarrow\mathscr{G}f for the groups ℛ \mathscr{R} , 𝒦 , {\mathscr{K}}\!, 𝒜 \mathscr{A} . Submodules of T 𝒢 ​ f T_{\mathscr{G}}f ensure (shifted) submodules of 𝒢 ​ f \mathscr{G}f . For the 𝒜 \mathscr{A} -equivalence this extends (and strengthens) various classical results of Mather, Gaffney, du Plessis, and others. • Given a filtration on the space of maps one has the filtration on the group, 𝒢 ( ∙ ) \mathscr{G}^{(\bullet)} , and on the tangent space, T 𝒢 ( ∙ ) T_{\mathscr{G}^{(\bullet)}} . We establish the criteria of type “ T 𝒢 ( j ) ​ f T_{\mathscr{G}^{(j)}}f vs 𝒢 ( j ) ​ f \mathscr{G}^{(j)}f ” in their strongest form, for arbitrary base field/ring, provided the characteristic is zero or high for a given f f . This brings the “inevitably weaker” results of c ​ h ​ a ​ r > 0 char>0 to the level of c ​ h ​ a ​ r = 0 . char=0. As an auxiliary step, important on its own, we develop the mixed-module structure of the tangent space T 𝒜 ​ f T_{\mathscr{A}}f and establish various properties of the annihilator ideal 𝔞 𝒜 \mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}} , defining the instability locus of the map.



1.
Introduction

1.1.

Consider
\Bbbk
-analytic map-germs,
f:(\Bbbk^{n},o){\to}(\Bbbk^{p},o)
, here
\Bbbk
is
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}.

Whitney’s initial results on stable maps were greatly developed and extended by Thom, Mather and many others.
(See e.g.
[
A.G.L.V.
]
,
[
Martinet
]
,
[
Mon. Nuñ.-Bal.
]
.)
The maps are traditionally studied up to
the left-right (
\mathscr{A}
) equivalence, with the auxiliary right (
\mathscr{R}
) and contact (
{\mathscr{K}}\!
) equivalences. The first questions are about the group-orbits,
\mathscr{G}f\subset\rm{Maps}\big((\Bbbk^{n},o),\!(\Bbbk^{p},o)\big)
,
in particular the finite determinacy.

The classical methods relied heavily on vector fields integration. This chained the whole theory to the
\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
-analytic (or
C^{r}
-differentiable) setting. Even the Nash function-germs,
\mathbb{R}\langle x\rangle,
could not be treated.

The study of
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!
-equivalences by purely algebraic methods (in zero and positive characteristic) has begun in 80’s, e.g.
[
Greuel-Kröning.90
]
.
This was motivated by the study of more complicated (e.g. non-isolated) singularities, by the applications in Algebraic Geometry/Commutative Algebra, and by the need to enable calculations in Computer Algebra.
By now numerous results are available,
[
Bou.Gr.Ma.12
]
,
[
Bou.Gr.Ma.11
]
,

[
Greuel-Nguyen.16
]
,
[
Nguyen.19
]
,
[
Greuel-Pham.19
]
,
[
B.K.16
]
,
[
B.G.K.22
]
.
The results in positive characteristic are (non-surprisingly) weaker than those in characteristic zero.

The
\mathscr{A}
-case (the most important for the study of maps) is essentially more complicated than the (auxiliary)
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!
-cases.
Even the case of characteristic zero was untouched.
The
\mathscr{A}
-orbits could not be studied by the (algebraic) methods of the previous papers, e.g. because of the following difficulties.

•

The group-action
\mathscr{A}\circlearrowright\rm{Maps}\big((\Bbbk^{n},o),\!(\Bbbk^{p},o)\big)
is neither additive, nor
\Bbbk
-multiplicative,
unlike the actions
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!^{lin}\circlearrowright\rm{Maps}\big((\Bbbk^{n},o),\!(\Bbbk^{p},o)\big)
.

•

The tangent space
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
is not an
\mathcal{O}_{(\Bbbk^{n},o)}
-module, it is only a “mixed
(\mathcal{O}_{(\Bbbk^{n},o)},\mathcal{O}_{(\Bbbk^{p},o)})
-module”.

•

The essential ingredient was the Thom-Levine lemma (“naturality of vector field integration”) on the solution of a certain differential equation. Its algebraic version was absent.

•

The classical Artin approximation (so helpful for
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!
-equivalences) was absent (until recently) for the left-right equivalence.
(See §
2.3
.v for more detail.)

1.2.
The results

(The detailed description is in §
1.6
)
We study maps of germs,
Maps(X,(\Bbbk^{p},o)),
of arbitrary characteristic. Here
X=Spec(R_{X})
and
(\Bbbk^{p},o)=Spec(R_{Y})
are formal/
\Bbbk
-analytic/
\Bbbk
-Nash germs of schemes. More precisely,
(R_{X},R_{Y})
is one
of the pairs
({\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk[[x]]$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},\Bbbk[[y]])
,
({\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},\Bbbk\{y\})
,
({\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},\Bbbk\langle y\rangle),

see §
2.1
.ii. Here
\Bbbk
is any field or an excellent Henselian ring. (The later case is important for deformations.)

The space of maps is an
R_{X}
-module,
Maps(X,(\Bbbk^{p},o)):=Hom(R_{(\Bbbk^{p},o)},R_{X})\cong{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
, for the maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}\subset R_{X}.

Fix a map
f\in{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
and let
\mathscr{G}
be one of the groups
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!,\mathscr{A}.
Our results are of two types: conditions to ensure
the large orbit
\mathscr{G}f
(in terms of the tangent space
T_{\mathscr{G}}f
), and the structure of the tangent space
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
.

1.2.1.
The
\mathscr{G}
-implicit function theorems

Fix an ideal
\mathfrak{a}\subset R_{X}.
Theorems
4.1
,
5.1
,
7.4
read (roughly):

(1)

If
\mathfrak{a}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}\subseteq T_{\mathscr{G}}f
then
\mathscr{G}f\supseteq\{f\}+\mathfrak{a}^{2}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

This is characteristic-free, and with no restrictions on the critical/singular/instability locus of the map
f.

In many case
\mathfrak{a}
can be chosen as an ideal that defines the non-reduced critical/singular/instability locus (with the natural scheme structure).
One can say roughly:
f
is
\mathscr{G}
-determined by its “2-jet” on the critical/singular/instability locus. (The “2-jet” means the image in
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$R_{X}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$\mathfrak{a}^{2}$}\right.}
) Some results of this type are known for
\mathfrak{a}={\mathfrak{m}}^{d}
and
R_{X}=\mathbb{C}\{x\}
, e.g.

[
Mon. Nuñ.-Bal.
, Theorem 6.2, pg. 182]
.
The proofs were heavily analytic. Even the characteristic zero case is totally new.

1.2.2.
The filtration criteria, “
T_{\mathscr{G}^{(j)}}f
vs
\mathscr{G}^{(j)}f
”

Fix an ideal
I\subset R_{X}
, then the space of maps acquires the filtration
I^{\bullet}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
. This induces filtrations on the group,
\mathscr{G}^{\bullet}
, and on the tangent space,
T_{\mathscr{G}^{\bullet}}
. Theorems
4.5
,
5.4
,
7.8
read (roughly):

(2)

\mathscr{G}^{(j)}f\supseteq\{f\}+I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
if and only if
T_{\mathscr{G}^{(j)}}f\supseteq I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

In this case we assume: either
char(\Bbbk)=0
or
char(\Bbbk)>\frac{d-ord(f)}{j}.

For
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!
-equivalences this result is known in characteristic zero,
[
B.G.K.22
]
, while for
char(\Bbbk)>\frac{d-ord(f)}{j}
it strengthens the known results “by a factor of 2”. We remark, that the previous proofs of the “only if”-part (in
char>0
) needed an additional assumption “
\Bbbk
is infinite” and non-trivial deformation/specialization arguments,
[
Greuel-Pham.19
]
.

For
\mathscr{A}
-equivalence this was known only in the analytic/smooth-cases,
R_{X}=\mathbb{C}\{x\},\mathbb{R}\{x\},C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},o),
with heavily analytic proofs,
[
B.dP.W.87
]
. Even the zero characteristic case is new.

A remark, the ideals
\mathfrak{a},I
in (
1
) and (
2
) are not necessarily
{\mathfrak{m}}
-primary. I.e. there is no restriction on the dimension of the critical/singular/instability loci.

For many more results see §
1.6
.

1.2.3.
The structure of
T_{\mathscr{A}}f

The tangent spaces
T_{\mathscr{R}}f,T_{\mathscr{K}}\!f\subseteq R^{\oplus p}_{X}
are
R_{X}
-submodules. Their properties can be addressed via the standard commutative/homological algebra. The tangent space
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
is not an
R_{X}
-module, it is only an
R_{Y}
-module
(and not finitely-generated when
dim(X)>p
or when
f
is not
\mathscr{A}
-finite). The classical Nakayama/Artin-Rees lemmas do not hold for
T_{\mathscr{A}}f.

We establish their substitutions and numerous “little tools” in §
6
. (See §
1.6
for the list of results.)

In particular we prove: if
f
is a morphism of “finite singularity type”, then the image tangent space
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
is a
finitely generated
module over certain extension of
R_{(\Bbbk^{p},o)},
namely: the Noetherian, local ring
f^{\sharp}R_{(\Bbbk^{p},o)}+\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}}.

1.3.
The methods

The proofs of theorems
4.1
,
5.1
are heavily based on the implicit function arguments (hence their name “
\mathscr{G}
-implicit function theorem”).
More precisely, we use a version of Newton lemma of the type of
[
Bourbaki.CA
]
-
[
Tougeron.66
]
.
The proof of (the main) theorem
7.4
is much more complicated and demands additional tools. (Weierstraß division, arc-solution, Kostant-Rosenlicht theorem, and a special argument when the field

{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{${\mathfrak{m}}_{\Bbbk}$}\right.}
is not algebraically closed.)

The proofs of theorems
4.5
,
5.4
,
7.8
are based on the
algebraic
Thom-Levine lemma (§
3.5
) and the
full

Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff expansion.

1.4.

Establishing these “fundamentals” of
\mathscr{A}
-equivalence opens the way to further results.
In this paper I give only the basic/simplest examples, see §
1.6
.
More serious applications contain (in arbitrary characteristic) the theory of unfoldings, the theory of stable maps, Mather-Yau/Gaffney-Hauser theorems, algebraization of (non-finitely-determined) maps,
[
Kerner.22
]
.
Moreover, the new criteria on the orbit
\mathscr{A}f
and on the structure of the tangent space
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
seem useful for the classification/study of
\mathscr{A}
-simple (
\mathbb{C}
-analytic) maps.

A remark: the proofs do not use the notion of “geometric equivalence” of
[
Damon.84
]
, the methods seem applicable to the orbit-study of various other groups, e.g. volume preserving/symplectic equivalences,
[
Domitrz-Rieger.09
]
.

1.5.
Acknowledgement

Thanks are to A. F. Boix, G.-M. Greuel, D. Mond, M. A. S. Ruas for important advices.
I was heavily influenced by their papers and also by those of T. Gaffney, A. du Plessis, L. Wilson.

Special thanks are to M. Borovoi and Z. Rosegarten for their help with Kostant-Rosenlicht issues, §
7.1
.

Finally I thank the referee of
[
B.G.K.22
]
who urged me to treat the
\mathscr{A}
-case (and not only
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!
).

1.6.
The detailed structure, results, and contents of the paper

​§2​

is about the general preliminaries, to make the paper self-contained.

​§2.1​

fixes the rings we work with, i.e.
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk[\![x]\!]$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},

{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},

{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}.

​§2.2​

identifies the space of maps,
Maps(X,(\Bbbk^{p},o))={\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
, the isomorphism of
R_{X}
-modules.

​§2.3​

recalls “the basic tools”,
Weierstraß division and finiteness,
the full Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula, Artin approximation for
\Bbbk
-analytic/
\Bbbk
-Nash equations and for the left-right equivalence.

​§2.4​

is about the “local coordinate changes” (i.e. the ring automorphisms
Aut_{X}:=Aut_{\Bbbk}(R_{X})
) and
the “germs of vector fields” (i.e. the derivations
Der_{X}:=Der_{\Bbbk}(R_{X})
). We recall the corresponding filtrations, on the group,
\{Aut^{\bullet}_{X}\}
,
and on the module,
\{Der^{\bullet}_{X}\}
.

​§2.5​

recalls the exp/log maps,
Der_{X}\leftrightarrows Aut_{X}
and their truncated versions,
jet_{N}Exp,

jet_{N}Ln
.
In zero or high characteristic they substitute the integration of vector fields.

​§3​

sets-up the basic notions of
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!,\mathscr{A}
equivalence over
\Bbbk.
The cases
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!
are “known in some sense”,

[
B.K.16
]
,
[
B.G.K.22
]
, but the
\mathscr{A}
-case is new.

​§3.1​

The group-actions on the space of maps,
\mathscr{G}\circlearrowright Maps(X,(\Bbbk^{p},o))={\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
, for
\mathscr{G}
one of the groups
\mathscr{R},\mathscr{L},\mathscr{C},{\mathscr{K}}\!,\mathscr{A}:=\mathscr{L}\times\mathscr{R}
, are defined as in the classical case.

​§3.2​

The (extended) image tangent spaces
T_{\mathscr{G}}f\subset R^{\oplus p}_{X}
are defined via the module(s) of derivations, e.g.

T_{\mathscr{R}}f:=Der_{X}(f),

T_{\mathscr{K}}\!f:=T_{\mathscr{R}}f+(f)\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

As in the classical case,
T_{\mathscr{R}}f,

T_{\mathscr{K}}\!f
are
R_{X}
-submodules of
R^{\oplus p}_{X}
, while
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
is only a “mixed” module.

​§3.3​

Fix an ideal
I\subset R_{X}
to get the filtration on the space of maps,
I^{\bullet}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}.
It induces (natural) filtrations of the group,
\mathscr{G}\rhd\mathscr{G}^{(\bullet)}
, and of the tangent space,

T_{\mathscr{G}}\supseteq T_{\mathscr{G}^{(\bullet)}}.

A little twist: the filtrations
\mathscr{A}^{\bullet}
,
T_{\mathscr{A}^{\bullet}}
and
{\mathscr{K}}\!^{\bullet}
,
T_{{\mathscr{K}}\!^{\bullet}}
could be also defined in another way.
Lemma
3.4
ensures: these distinct definitions are compatible
when
\Bbbk
is an infinite field.

​§3.4​

This filtration
I^{\bullet}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
defines the (filtration) topology on
R^{\oplus p}_{X}
. The tangent space
T_{\mathscr{G}}f
, and the orbit
\mathscr{G}f
, are closed in this topology for the groups
\mathscr{G}=\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!
.
The (standard) proof is based on the Artin-Rees lemma and the classical Artin approximation.

The
\mathscr{A}
-case is more complicated (in the absence of standard methods), see §
6.5
.

​§3.5​

gives the algebraic version of the classical Thom-Levine lemma “Naturality of vector fields integration”.
That basic lemma was of key importance for
\mathscr{A}
-equivalence in the
\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
-analytic cases.
And the algebraic version is used through the paper (when the characteristic is zero or high enough) in the same way.

​§3.6​

introduces the annihilator ideal
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{G}}:=Ann[T^{1}_{\mathscr{G}}f]\subset R_{X}
. Here
T^{1}_{\mathscr{G}}f:={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$R^{\oplus p}_{X}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$T_{\mathscr{G}}f$}\right.}
,
this is the tangent space to
\mathscr{G}
-miniversal unfoldings, when
f
is
\mathscr{G}
-finite.
For functions (
p=1
) on smooth germs, i.e.
(\Bbbk^{n},o)\to(\Bbbk^{1},o)
, these are the classical Jacobian/Tjurina ideals,
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{R}}=Jac(f)
,
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{K}}\!=Jac(f)+(f)
.
When the source
X
is singular, one gets Bruce-Roberts’ versions of these ideals.

More generally (for
p\geq 1
), the ideal
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{R}}
defines the critical locus of a map,
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{K}}\!
defines the singular locus,
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}}
defines
the instability locus.
A remark:
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{G}}
is defined as the annihilator ideal, not as the Fitting ideal.

The annihilators
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{R}},\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{K}}\!
are well known, but
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}}
seems to be not studied previously.

Lemma
3.15
extends the classical fact (from
\Bbbk=\mathbb{C}
to local Henselian rings): the restriction
f|_{Crit(f)}:Crit(f)\to(\Bbbk^{p},o)
is a
finite morphism iff
f
is
{\mathscr{K}}\!
-finite iff
T^{1}_{\mathscr{R}}f
is a f.g. module over
R_{Y}
.

​§4​

is the first warmup, addressing the orbits of the group
\mathscr{R}.

​​§4.1​

contains the “
\mathscr{R}
-implicit function theorem
”. It puts a large part of the tangent space
into the group-orbit:
\mathscr{R}f\supseteq\{f\}+\mathfrak{a}\cdot T_{\mathscr{R}}f
. This statement (and its proof) is characteristic-free.

The proof goes in the style of implicit function theorem of
[
Bourbaki.CA
]
and
[
Tougeron.66
]
.

​​§4.2​

Numerous examples show that this bound is much stronger than the known bounds (even in the
\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
-cases).
As a trivial corollary we get the Morse lemma over an arbitrary field.

A geometric corollary: the map
f
is
\mathscr{R}
-determined by its “2-jet” on the critical locus,
\mathscr{R}f\supseteq\{f\}+\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{R}}^{2}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
.

​​§4.3​

is the filtration criterion for
\mathscr{R}
-orbits via tangent spaces: “
\mathscr{R}^{(j)}f\!\supseteq\!\{f\}\!+\!I^{d}\!\cdot\!R^{\oplus p}_{X}
iff
T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}f\!\supseteq\!I^{d}\!\cdot\!R^{\oplus p}_{X}
”.

(In positive characteristic the integers
j,d
are restricted by the condition
{d-ord(f)}<j\cdot char(\Bbbk)
, i.e. the
characteristic must be large enough for a given
j,d
.)

A statement of this type has first appeared in
[
B.dP.W.87
]
for
\Bbbk=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
, and today it is known in characteristic zero.
In positive characteristic it strengthens the known criteria,
[
Greuel-Pham.19
]
,
[
B.G.K.22
]
,
all being of type
“If
T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}f\supseteq I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
then

\mathscr{R}^{(j+d)}f\supseteq\{f\}+I^{2d-ord(f)+1}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
”.

To emphasize, the new statement is of “iff” type.

The proof uses the truncated exp/ln operators,
jet_{N}(Exp),

jet_{N}(Ln).

​§5​

is the second warmup, addressing the
{\mathscr{K}}\!
-equivalence.

​§5.1​

contains
the “
{\mathscr{K}}\!
-implicit function theorem
”. It puts a large part of the tangent space
into the orbit (roughly):
{\mathscr{K}}\!f\supseteq\{f\}+(\mathfrak{a}^{2}+{\mathfrak{m}}(f))\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
. As in the
\mathscr{R}
-case, this is characteristic-free and
much stronger than the known bounds (we give several examples).

A geometric corollary: the map
f
is
{\mathscr{K}}\!
-determined by its “2-jet” on the singular locus,
{\mathscr{K}}\!f\supseteq\{f\}+\mathfrak{a}^{2}_{\mathscr{K}}\!\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
.

​§5.2​

is an immediate application: the
{\mathscr{K}}\!
-orbit of
a reduced complete intersection curve germ
(C,o)\!\subset\!(\Bbbk^{n},o)
, in terms of the semi-group of values.

​§5.3​

is the filtration criterion for
{\mathscr{K}}\!
-orbits: “
{\mathscr{K}}\!^{(j)}f\supseteq\{f\}+I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
iff
T_{{\mathscr{K}}\!^{(j)}}f\supseteq I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
”.
As in the
\mathscr{R}
-case, for
char(\Bbbk)\!>\!0
the characteristic is restricted by the condition
d\!-\!ord(f)\!<\!j\!\cdot\!char(\Bbbk)
.

As in the
\mathscr{R}
-case, this statement is known in characteristic zero, but in positive (high enough) characteristic it strengthens the known criteria “by a factor of 2”.

The proof uses (besides the truncated maps
jet_{N}(Exp)
,
jet(Ln)
) the full
Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff expansion, §
2.3
.iv.
(We bound the prime numbers appearing in the denominators.)

The
\mathscr{A}
-equivalence is much more complicated. (Recall that the
{\mathscr{K}}\!
-equivalence was initially introduced as an auxiliary,
simpler version of
\mathscr{A}
-equivalence.) A difficulty of
\mathscr{A}
(besides those mentioned in §1.1) is that the classical
arguments were heavily based on Thom-Levine’s lemma, which holds only in zero or high characteristic, and on Mather-Malgrange preparation theorem, which assumes that
T_{\mathscr{G}}f
is an
R_{X}
-module.
Without these results one needs special tools.

The next two sections are the core of the paper.

​§6​

is “The basic theory of
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
”.

​§6.1​

The annihilator ideal
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}}\!=\!Ann[T^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}f]\subset R_{X}
(for
T^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}f:={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$R^{\oplus p}_{X}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$T_{\mathscr{A}}f$}\right.}
) is much more delicate than the corresponding annihilators
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{R}},\!\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{K}}\!.
We establish basic properties of
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}}.

​§6.2​

The (extended) tangent space
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
is not an
R_{X}
-module. Considered as an
R_{Y}
-module it is often not finitely-generated (e.g. when
dim(X)>p
).
We prove that
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
is a module over the extended ring
f^{\#}(R_{Y})+\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}}
. Moreover,
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
is finitely-generated over
f^{\#}(R_{Y})+\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}}

in the most important case,
“
f
is of finite singularity type” (i.e.
f
is
{\mathscr{K}}\!
-finite, i.e. the germ
V(f)\subset X
has an isolated singularity).

​§6.3​

As
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
is not an
R_{X}
-module, one does not have the usual Nakayama statement, “
M\subseteq T_{\mathscr{A}}f+{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot M
implies
M\subseteq T_{\mathscr{A}}f
.”
We give weaker versions.

​§6.4​

Similarly the assumption “
T_{\mathscr{A}^{(j)}}f\supseteq I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
” does not imply
``T_{\mathscr{A}^{(j+1)}}f\supseteq I^{d+1}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}".

Yet, we establish weaker statements of Artin-Rees type.

​§6.5​

Unlike the tangent spaces
T_{\mathscr{R}}f,T_{\mathscr{K}}\!f
, the space
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
is not “obviously filtration-closed”.
One cannot deduce
\overline{T_{\mathscr{A}}f}=T_{\mathscr{A}}f
directly, just from the faithfulness/exactness of the completion functor
\lim\limits_{\leftarrow}{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$R_{X}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$I^{\bullet}$}\right.}\otimes
.
And one cannot apply the Artin approximation, as this is an “inverse Artin problem”.
Yet, one has
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}}\cdot T_{\mathscr{A}}f=\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}}\cdot\overline{T_{\mathscr{A}}f}.
For maps of finite singularity type we prove:
T_{\mathscr{A}}f=\overline{T_{\mathscr{A}}f}.

​§7​

is about large modules inside
\mathscr{A}
-orbits.

​§7.1​

recalls the auxiliary result, the Kostant-Rosenlicht theorem (“Orbits of unipotent algebraic groups are closed”) over algebraically-closed fields.
I add also an extension for arbitrary fields of characteristic zero (by M. Borovoi) and an example (by Z.Rosegarten) showing the pathology in positive characteristic, even when the field
\Bbbk
is perfect.

​§7.2​

contains the “
\mathscr{A}
-implicit function theorem
”,
\mathscr{A}f\supseteq\{f\}+\mathfrak{a}^{2}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}+T_{\mathscr{L}^{(1)}}f
.
This is the main (and hardest) result of the paper.
The proof is essentially more complicated than those in the
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!
-cases.

​i.​​

The condition
\mathscr{A}f\ni f+g
is not an implicit function equation (unlike the conditions
\mathscr{R}f\ni f+g
,
{\mathscr{K}}\!f\ni f+g
).
Yet, we convert it to an implicit function equation, modulo terms of high enough order.
In this process we pass from the source
X
to the target
(\Bbbk^{p},o),

using the Weierstraß division.

​ii.​​

We obtain an arc solution of a system, i.e. a solution over the ring
R_{Y}[[t]]
.
Then one passes to the finite jets, and finds the ordinary solution for infinite number of
t
-values.

​iii.​​

Use Zariski-closedness of the orbits of unipotent algebraic groups (by Kostant-Rosenlicht).
If the field
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{${\mathfrak{m}}_{\Bbbk}$}\right.}
is not algebraically-closed, then one invokes an argument of Galois-cohomology.

​§7.3​

The first immediate corollary is for the
\mathscr{A}
-orbits of stable maps:
\mathscr{A}f\supseteq\{f\}+\mathfrak{a}^{2}_{{\mathscr{K}}\!^{(o)}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
. This bound is completely new even in the
classical case
R_{X}=\mathbb{C}\{x\},\mathbb{R}\{x\}
, and is much stronger than the known bounds.
In fact this bound holds for a broader class of maps, a sufficient assumption is:
T_{\mathscr{A}^{(o)}}f=T_{{\mathscr{K}}\!^{(o)}}f,
i.e.
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}^{(o)}}=\mathfrak{a}_{{\mathscr{K}}\!^{(o)}}.

Then come other examples showing how to apply this
\mathscr{A}
-
IFT
theorem and how to extend numerous classical results to arbitrary characteristic.
E.g. any
\mathscr{A}
-finite map is
\mathscr{A}
-finitely determined, various explicit determinacy bounds, and so on.

​§7.4​

contains the filtration criterion,
“
\mathscr{A}^{(j)}f\!\supseteq\!\{f\}\!+\!I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
iff
T_{\mathscr{A}^{(j)}}f\!\supseteq\!I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
”.
As in the
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!
-cases, for
char(\Bbbk)\!>\!0
the integers
j,d
are restricted by the characteristic,

2d\!-\!1\!-\!ord(f)\!<\!j\!\cdot\!char(\Bbbk).

This statement is known for
R_{X}=\Bbbk\{x\}
,
\Bbbk\in\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
, but is completely new in zero/positive characteristic.
As in the
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!
-case, the proof uses the truncated maps
jet_{N}(Exp),

jet_{N}(Ln).
But the additional (essential) ingredient is the algebraic Thom-Levine lemma of §
3.5
.

​§7.5​

gives the geometric criterion of determinacy:
f
is determined by its finite jet along the instability locus in the target,

f(V(\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}}))
. (Or, along the locus
f^{-1}(f(V(\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}})))\cap Crit(f)
.)
For
\mathscr{A}
-finite maps (i.e.
V(\mathfrak{a}_{\mathscr{A}})
is a point) this extends the classical Mather-Gaffney criterion.
Otherwise this seems to be new even in the
\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
-cases.

2.
Notations, conventions, preliminaries

2.1.
Local rings/germs of schemes

We study Maps(
X,(\Bbbk^{p},o)
), where
X=Spec(R_{X}),

(\Bbbk^{p},o)=Spec(R_{Y})
are germs of schemes over the base ring
\Bbbk.

In the simplest case
\Bbbk
is a field, classically
\Bbbk=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
. The case when
\Bbbk
is not a field is important for deformations/unfoldings, e.g.

\Bbbk=\tilde{\Bbbk}[[t]]
, resp.
\tilde{\Bbbk}\{t\},\tilde{\Bbbk}\langle t\rangle,

see
[
Kerner.22
]
.
Through the paper we use the multi-variables,

x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),

y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{p}),

t=(t_{1},\dots,t_{r}).

​
i.​

Denote by
\Bbbk^{\times}
the group of invertible elements of
\Bbbk
. Sometimes we impose the condition “
2,\dots,d\in\Bbbk^{\times}
”.
E.g. this holds if
\Bbbk\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
or
\Bbbk
contains a field of characteristic larger than
d
.

​
ii.​

(R_{X},{\mathfrak{m}}),
or just
(R,{\mathfrak{m}}),
is one of the following local
\Bbbk
-algebras.

•

The formal power series
R=\Bbbk[[x]],
or the quotient ring
R={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk[[x]]$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}.
Here
\Bbbk
is either a(ny) field or
a complete local (Noetherian) ring, e.g.
\Bbbk={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\tilde{\Bbbk}[[t]]$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$I$}\right.}.

Then
R
is complete with respect to the filtration
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\bullet}
.

•

The
\Bbbk
-analytic (locally convergent) power series
R=\Bbbk\{x\},
or the quotient ring
R={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
.
In this case
\Bbbk
is either a normed ring (e.g a normed field), complete with respect to its (non-discrete) norm, or
\Bbbk={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\tilde{\Bbbk}\{t\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$I$}\right.},
where
\tilde{\Bbbk}
is a normed ring (as before).
The relevant cases of complete normed fields are
\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

•

The algebraic power series
R=\Bbbk\langle x\rangle
(i.e. power series in
x
that satisfy monic polynomial equations over
\Bbbk[x]
)
or the quotient ring
R={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}.

Here
\Bbbk
is either a(ny) field or a local excellent Henselian ring.
Then
\Bbbk\langle x\rangle
is the Henselization of the ring
\Bbbk[x]
at the ideal
(x)
, constructed as the filtered colimit of étale
\Bbbk[x]
-algebras.
See
[
Moret-Bailly.21
]
for more detail.

The elements of
\mathbb{R}\langle x\rangle
are exactly the Nash germs, see e.g.
[
Ruiz
]
for the basic introduction.
Occasionally we call the elements of
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
“
\Bbbk
-Nash germs” rather than “algebraic germ”, to avoid any confusion with rings like
\Bbbk[x],

\Bbbk[x]_{\mathfrak{m}}.

The assumptions on
\Bbbk
(local complete, resp. normed and complete for its norm, resp. local excellent Henselian) are needed to ensure the
implicit function theorem, Weierstraß finiteness, and Artin approximation, see §
2.3
.

Denote the maximal ideal of
\Bbbk
by
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Bbbk}.

Occasionally we denote the image of
x
in
R
by the same letter. Thus the maximal ideal is
{\mathfrak{m}}={\mathfrak{m}}_{\Bbbk}+(x)\subset R.

If
\Bbbk
is a field then
{\mathfrak{m}}=(x).

The elements of
R
are represented by power series. Abusing notations we write
f(x),
to emphasize the dependence on
x.

​
iii.​

Geometrically the source of the map
X=Spec(R)\subseteq(\Bbbk^{n},o)
(or
X=Spec(R_{X})
) is a formal/
\Bbbk
-analytic/
\Bbbk
-Nash germ, and
x
denotes the local coordinates on the ambient space
(\Bbbk^{n},o).

The target of the map is
Y\!:=\!(\Bbbk^{p},o)\!:=\!Spec(R_{Y}),
the formal/
\Bbbk
-analytic/
\Bbbk
-Nash germ (corresponding to the type of
X
).
Namely,
R_{Y}
is one of the rings

{\Bbbk[[y]]},

{\Bbbk\{y\}},

{\Bbbk\langle y\rangle},
with
\Bbbk
a field or a ring, as before.

Below we use
Y
and
(\Bbbk^{p},o)
interchangeably.

​
iv.​

Through the paper we have to compose elements
f(z)\in R[[z]]
with
g\in R.
For
g\in{\mathfrak{m}}\subset R
the element
f\circ g\in{\widehat{R}}
is well defined. For
g\!\in\!R\!\setminus\!{\mathfrak{m}}
the element
f\!\circ\!g
can be not well defined. But in the proof of theorems
4.1
,
5.1
,
7.4

the series
f
are of special form,
f(z)\!\in\!R[[{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot z]][z]
, i.e.
f
is a polynomial in
z
, whose coefficients are power series in
{\mathfrak{m}}\!\cdot\!z
.
In this case the composition
f\!\circ\!g
is well defined for any
g\!\in\!R.

​
v.​

Take a tuple of elements
f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{p})\in R^{\oplus p}
and an ideal
I\subset R.
Denote the
I
-order of the ideal
(f)\subset R
by
ord(f)
. Namely,
(f)\subseteq I^{ord(f)}
and
(f)\not\subseteq I^{ord(f)+1}
.
Sometimes we use the ideal
I^{ord(f)-2}
. If
ord(f)\leq 2
or
I=R_{X}
we put
I^{ord(f)-2}:=R_{X}
.

​
vi.​

Recall the general fact: any
R_{X}
-submodule
M\subseteq R^{\oplus p}_{X}
is finitely generated. Proof:
R^{\oplus p}_{X}
itself is a f.g. module over the Noetherian ring
R_{X}
,
therefore
R^{\oplus p}_{X}
is a Noetherian module. Hence
M
is f.g.

​
vii.​

We consider only local homomorphisms of local
\Bbbk
-algebras, i.e.
\phi:R_{Y}\to R_{X}
with
\phi({\mathfrak{m}}_{Y})\subseteq{\mathfrak{m}}_{X}.
Such homomorphisms are necessarily Krull-continuous, see e.g. Lemma 15.36.2 of
[
Stacks
]
. Therefore they act by substitution,
\phi(f(y))=f(\phi(y)).

In particular, the homeomorphism
\phi
is determined by its action on the generators
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{p}).

2.2.
The identification
\mathbf{Maps(X,\!Y)={\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}}
for
Y=(\Bbbk^{p},o)

A map of germs
f:X\to Y
is defined algebraically by the (local) homomorphism of
\Bbbk
-algebras,
f^{\sharp}\in Hom(R_{Y},R_{X}).

Fix generators
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{p})
in
R_{Y}.

Any homomorphism
R_{Y}\to R_{X}
is determined by the image of
y
in
{\mathfrak{m}}\subset R_{X},
see §
2.1
.vii.
Vice-versa, any such image extends to a homomorphism.
Accordingly we identify
Maps(X,\!Y)\!=\!Hom_{\Bbbk}(R_{Y},R_{X})\!=\!{\mathfrak{m}}\!\cdot\!R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

This identification equips the set
Maps(X,\!Y)
with the
R_{X}
-module structure.

If
\Bbbk
is a field then
{\mathfrak{m}}=(x)\subset R_{X}
and the maps fix the origin,
X\ni o\to o\in(\Bbbk^{p},o)
.
If
\Bbbk
is a local ring then
{\mathfrak{m}}={\mathfrak{m}}_{\Bbbk}+(x)
, and
Maps(X,\!Y)
can be interpreted as families of maps, possibly displacing the origin.

Given a map
f\in Maps(X,\!Y)={\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}\subset R^{\oplus p}_{X},
the space
R^{\oplus p}_{X}
becomes a (not finitely-generated)
R_{Y}
-module via the composition,

(3)

q(y)\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}:=f^{\#}(q(y))\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}:=q(f_{1},\dots,f_{p})\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

We have the
R_{Y}
-submodule
f^{\#}(R^{\oplus p}_{Y})\subseteq f^{*}(R^{\oplus p}_{Y})=R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

Here
f^{\#}(y)\subseteq f^{*}(y)=(f)\subset R_{X}
, and the first inclusion is usually proper.

Recall the classical notations,
\omega f({\mathfrak{m}}_{p})
for
f^{\#}(y)
, and
f^{*}({\mathfrak{m}}_{p})
for
f^{*}(y)
,
[
Mon. Nuñ.-Bal.
]
.

2.3.
The basic tools

(They are used repeatedly in §
4
-
7
.)

​
i.​

Implicit function theorem with unit linear part,
\mathbf{IFT_{1\hskip-2.84544pt\rm I}}
.

We often have to resolve implicit function equations of type
z=f(z)
for a vector of (formal/analytic/algebraic) power series:

(4)

f(z)\in({\mathfrak{m}}+(z)^{2})\cdot R[[z]]^{\oplus p},\quad\text{ resp. }f(z)\in({\mathfrak{m}}+(z)^{2})\cdot R\{z\}^{\oplus p},\quad\text{ resp. }f(z)\in({\mathfrak{m}}+(z)^{2})\cdot R\langle z\rangle^{\oplus p}.

Here
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{p})
are the unknowns and the equations are non-polynomial in
z.
We look for the solution
z\in{\mathfrak{m}}\subset R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

There exists the unique formal solution
\hat{z}(x)\in{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot\hat{R}^{\oplus p}.

It is obtained, e.g. by the order-by-order procedure.

The implicit function theorem holds for our rings and ensures: this formal solution belongs to
R.

•

For the ring
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk[[x]]$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
, with
\Bbbk
any complete local ring, this statement is trivial.

•

IFT_{1\hskip-2.84544pt\rm I}
holds in the rings
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk[[x]]$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
,
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
,
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
, for any (normed) field
\Bbbk
, see example 2.2 of

[
B.G.K.22
]
.

•

IFT_{1\hskip-2.84544pt\rm I}
holds in the ring ​
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
​ for ​
\Bbbk
​ a normed ring, complete with respect to its norm,
[
Abhyankar
, pg.84]
.

•

IFT_{1\hskip-2.84544pt\rm I}
holds in the ring
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
, for
\Bbbk
a local, excellent, Henselian ring,
[
Lafon.67
]
, see also page 4 of
1
1

1

[
Denef-Lipshitz
]
assume
\Bbbk
a field or a DVR (and denote
\Bbbk
by
R
). But their proof uses only the Weierstraß preparation, which holds for
\Bbbk
-local, excellent, Henselian.

[
Denef-Lipshitz
]
.

Sometimes the system to resolve has the form
z=h(z)+c
, for a vector
c\in R^{\oplus p}
, where the entries of the vector
h(z)

belong to the subset
{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R[[{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot z]][z]\subset R[[z]]
.
This case is reduced to that of
z=f(z)
by the substitution
{\tilde{z}}=z-c
.

​
ii.​

Weierstraß division with remainder.
Take a local subring
R\subseteq\Bbbk[[x]]
, here
\Bbbk
is a local ring (e.g. a field).
The Weierstraß division holds in
R
if
for any
x_{n}
-regular element
f\in R
of
x_{n}
-order
d_{n}
, and any
g\in R
,
one can present
g=q\cdot f+r
, where
q\in R
and
r\in R\cap\Bbbk[[x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}]][x_{n}]
, with
deg_{x_{n}}r<d_{n}
.
This division with remainder holds in our rings of §
2.1
.ii.
For
\Bbbk\{x\}
see, e.g.
[
Abhyankar
, pg.75]
, for
\Bbbk\langle x\rangle
see
[
Lafon.65
]
. For more general results and further references see
[
Moret-Bailly.21
]
.

​
iii.​

Weierstraß finiteness condition
holds for a pair of local
\Bbbk
-algebras
R_{X},R_{Y}
if for any homomorphism
\phi:R_{Y}{\to}R_{X}

and any finitely generated
R_{X}
module
M
, with the quotient
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$M$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$\phi^{*}({\mathfrak{m}}_{Y})\cdot M$}\right.}
f.g. over
\Bbbk
, the module
M
is f.g. over
R_{Y}
.

This condition is implied by the Weierstraß division condition. In particular it holds for the pairs

({\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk[[x]]$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},\Bbbk[[y]])
,
({\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},\Bbbk\{y\})
,
({\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},\Bbbk\langle y\rangle)
.

A remark: the
C^{\infty}
-version of Weierstraß finiteness is a deep preparation theorem of Malgrange.

​
iv.​

Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula.
Suppose
\Bbbk\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
. Let
M
be a
\Bbbk
-module (possibly not finitely generated), with a filtration
M_{\bullet}
. Suppose
M
is
M_{\bullet}
-complete.
Suppose some operators (not necessarily
\Bbbk
-linear)
\xi,\eta\circlearrowright M
are filtration-nilpotent, i.e.
\xi,\eta(M_{\bullet})\subseteq M_{\bullet+1}
.
Then the operators
exp(\xi)
,
\exp(\eta)
,
\exp(\xi+\eta)
are defined on
M
. One has the classical relation

exp(\xi)exp(\eta)=exp(\sum^{\infty}_{l=1}p_{l}(\xi,\eta))
. Here
p_{l}(\xi,\eta)
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
l
in
non-commuting variables
\xi,\eta
.
The expansion begins as follows:

exp(\xi)\cdot exp(\eta)=exp\big(\xi+\eta+\frac{[\xi,\eta]}{2}+\frac{[\xi,[\xi,\eta]]-[\eta,[\xi,\eta]]}{12}+\cdots\big)
.
Below we use the full expansion, see, e.g.
[
Jacobson
, §5 of chapter 5]
or
[
Serre
, pg.29]
or
[
Bourbaki.Lie
, pg.160]
:

(5)

\sum^{\infty}_{l=1}p_{l}(\xi,\eta)=\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}\frac{(-1)^{i}}{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\dots\\
r_{i}+s_{i}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{[\xi^{r_{1}}\eta^{s_{1}}\cdots\xi^{r_{i}}\eta^{s_{i}}]}{\Big(\sum\limits^{i}_{\bullet=1}(r_{\bullet}+s_{\bullet})\Big)\Big(\mathop{\prod}\limits^{i}_{\bullet=1}r_{\bullet}!\Big)\Big(\mathop{\prod}\limits^{i}_{\bullet=1}s_{\bullet}!\Big)}.

Here
[\xi^{r_{1}}\eta^{s_{1}}\cdots\xi^{r_{i}}\eta^{s_{i}}]
is a certain combination of commutators with
\mathbb{Z}
coefficients.
Comparing the two sides we get
\sum^{i}_{\bullet=1}(r_{\bullet}+s_{\bullet})=l
and therefore:

(6)

p_{l}(\xi,\eta)=\frac{1}{l}\sum^{l}_{i=1}\frac{(-1)^{i}}{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{r_{\bullet}+s_{\bullet}>0\}_{\bullet}\\
\sum^{i}_{\bullet=1}(r_{\bullet}+s_{\bullet})=l\end{subarray}}\frac{[\xi^{r_{1}}\eta^{s_{1}}\cdots\xi^{r_{i}}\eta^{s_{i}}]}{\Big(\prod^{i}_{\bullet=1}r_{\bullet}!\Big)\Big(\prod^{i}_{\bullet=1}s_{\bullet}!\Big)}.

In particular, the rescaled polynomial
(l!)^{4}\cdot p_{l}(\xi,\eta)
has only integer coefficients.

We emphasize that the relation
exp(\xi)\cdot exp(\eta)=\dots
is purely formal,
and no linearity/algebraicity is assumed on the action
\xi,\eta\circlearrowright M
.

Recall that in the analytic case, i.e.
R={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
,
\Bbbk\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
, the series
\sum^{\infty}_{l=1}p_{l}(\xi,\eta)

can be nowhere convergent for some derivations
\xi,\eta\in Der_{\Bbbk}(R)
. However this series is locally convergent, i.e. the resulting
derivation is analytic,
\sum^{\infty}_{l=1}p_{l}(\xi,\eta)\in Der_{\Bbbk}(R)
, if one assumes the filtration-nilpotence,

\xi,\eta(M_{\bullet})\subseteq M_{\bullet+1}
.
Indeed, in this case we get the (filtration-unipotent) analytic automorphisms,
exp(\xi),exp(\eta)\in Aut_{\Bbbk}(R)
.
(See e.g. the proof of part 2 of Lemma 3.17 in
[
B.K.16
]
.) Their product is a filtration-unipotent automorphism as well,

exp(\sum^{\infty}_{l=1}p_{l}(\xi,\eta))\in Aut_{\Bbbk}(R)
. And therefore
\sum^{\infty}_{l=1}p_{l}(\xi,\eta)\in Der_{\Bbbk}(R)
.

​
v.​

Artin approximation.
Let
R_{X}
be one of
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk[[x]]$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
,
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
,
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
, with
\Bbbk

a local ring,
see §
2.1
.ii. Take a (finite) system
of implicit function equations,
F(y)=0
, here
F(y)\in R_{X}[[y]]^{\oplus N}
, resp.
R_{X}\{y\}^{\oplus N}
, resp.
R_{X}\langle y\rangle^{\oplus N}
.
Suppose we have an order-by-order solution, i.e. a sequence
{\tilde{y}}_{\bullet}(x)\in R^{\oplus q}_{X}
satisfying:

F({\tilde{y}}_{\bullet}(x))\in{\mathfrak{m}}^{\bullet}\cdot R^{\oplus N}_{X}
.
The Pfister-Popescu theorem,
[
Pfister-Popescu.75
]
(see also §2.4.1 of
[
B.G.K.22
]
), ensures a formal solution, i.e.
\hat{y}(x)\in{\widehat{R}}_{X}^{\oplus q}

satisfying
F(\hat{y}(x))=0
.

Suppose
R_{X}
is one of
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
,
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
, see §
2.1
.ii.
By the Artin approximation theorem,
[
Artin.68
]
,
[
Artin.69
]
, this formal solution is approximated by
R_{X}
-solutions. Namely, for each
d\geq 1

there exists
y_{d}(x)\in R_{X}^{\oplus q}
satisfying:
F(y_{d}(x))=0
and
\hat{y}(x)-y_{d}(x)\in(x)^{d}\cdot{\widehat{R}}_{X}^{\oplus q}
.

A remark: the general Artin approximation holds for
polynomial
equations over any Henselian local ring. In our case the equations
F(y)=0

are non-polynomial, they are (algebraic/analytic) power series.
Therefore we restrict to the particular rings
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
,
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
.
See
[
B.G.K.22
, §2.4.2]
,
[
Rond.18
]
,
[
Popescu.00
]
for more detail.

The equation of
\mathscr{A}
-equivalence is
\Phi_{Y}\circ f\circ\Phi_{X}^{-1}={\tilde{f}}
. This is not an implicit function equation.
And the Artin approximation does not work in the analytic case,
\Bbbk\{x\}
,
due to the example of
[
Gabrielov.73
]
, see
[
Shiota.98
, Fact.1.4]
.
The left-right version of the Artin approximation has been established:

•

R_{X}=\mathbb{R}\langle x\rangle,
for any
f
,
[
Shiota.98
, Fact.1.3]
,
[
Shiota.10
, Theorem.4]
.

•

R_{X}=\mathbb{R}\{x\},
for
f
of finite singularity type,
[
Shiota.98
, Fact.1.7]
.

The proof for
\mathbb{R}\langle x\rangle
is characteristic-free, and is valid over any field. ​But the proof for
\mathbb{R}\{x\}
is heavily based on the
C^{\infty}
-topology.
​These statements are extended in ​
[
Kerner.23
]
​ to the rings ​
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},\!{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}.

2.4.
​​Coordinate changes (ring automorphisms,
Aut_{X}
) and vector fields (ring derivations,
Der_{X}
)

​​
i.​

Denote by ​
Aut_{\Bbbk}(R)
​ the group of (local)
\Bbbk
-linear automorphisms of
R
. The elements of
R
are presentable by power series,
the automorphisms act
by substitution, ​
\phi(f(x))\!=\!f(\phi(x))
for
\phi(x)\!\in\!{\mathfrak{m}}.

See §
2.1
.

Geometrically (for ​
J\!=\!0
) these are “the coordinate changes” of
the germ
X\!=\!Spec(R).
​ See
[
B.G.K.22
, §2.3.2]
for more detail.
Because of this identification we abbreviate
Aut_{\Bbbk}(R)
to
Aut_{X}
.

​​
ii.​

Denote by
Der_{\Bbbk}(R)\subset End_{\Bbbk}(R)
the set of
\Bbbk
-linear derivations. This is an
R
-module.
Geometrically this is the module of germs of vector fields on
X=Spec(R)
, classically
denoted by
\Theta_{X}
.

For
R=\Bbbk[[x]]
,
\Bbbk\{x\}
,
\Bbbk\langle x\rangle
this module is generated by the partials

Der_{\Bbbk}(R)=R\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\rangle,
see e.g.
[
Matsumura
]
Theorem 30.6
More generally, for
R={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$S$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
with
S=\Bbbk[[x]],\Bbbk\{x\},\Bbbk\langle x\rangle
, we have the presentation

(7)

J\cdot Der_{\Bbbk}(S)\to DerLog(J)\to Der_{\Bbbk}(R)\to 0.

Here
DerLog(J)=\{\sum c_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}|\ \sum c_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}(J)\subseteq J\}\subset Der_{\Bbbk}(S)

is the module of log-derivations.
Its elements correspond to
the vector fields tangent to the subgerm
V(J)\subset(\Bbbk^{n},o)
.

Denoting
X=Spec(R)
we abbreviate
Der_{\Bbbk}(R)
to
Der_{X}
and
End_{\Bbbk}(R)
to
End_{X}
.

Applying derivations to an element ​
f\!\in\!R^{\oplus p}
​ we get the ​(finitely generated)​ submodule ​
Der_{X}(f)\!\subseteq\!R^{\oplus p}.

​​
iii.​

In
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{C}
-analytic (or
C^{r}
-) geometry one integrates vector fields to flows. Taking a flow at time
t=1
one gets the map
\Theta_{X}\to Aut_{X}
.
Over an arbitrary ring
\Bbbk
(without any topology/integration) the maps
Der_{X}\rightleftarrows Aut_{X}
are constructed in §
2.5
,
using the following filtration-topology.
(For more detail see
[
B.G.K.22
, §2.2.3]
.)

Fix a descending filtration by ideals,
R=I_{0}\supset I_{1}\supset\cdots
,
with
\cap I_{j}=(0)
. This defines the filtration topology on
R
.
Accordingly, for each
j\geq 0
, one considers the coordinate changes that are identity modulo higher order terms:

(8)

Aut^{(j)}_{X}:=\{\Phi\in Aut_{X}|\quad\Phi(I_{d})=I_{d}\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\Phi|_{{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$I_{d}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$I_{d+j}$}\right.}}=Id|_{{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$I_{d}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$I_{d+j}$}\right.}},\ \forall\ d\geq 1\}.

Thus
Aut^{(0)}_{X}
consists of automorphisms that preserve the filtration, while
Aut^{(1)}_{X}
consists of
“topologically unipotent” automorphism.
For the particular filtration
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\bullet}\subset R

one has
Aut_{X}=Aut^{(0)}_{X}
.
The sets
Aut^{(j)}_{X}
are normal subgroups, and we get the filtration

Aut^{(0)}_{X}\trianglerighteq Aut^{(1)}_{X}\triangleright\cdots
.

Similarly we consider (for every
j\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}
) “topologically nilpotent”
\Bbbk
-linear endomorphisms and derivations.

•

End^{(j)}_{\Bbbk}(R)\!:=\!\{\phi\!\in\!End_{\Bbbk}(R)|\ \phi(I_{d})\!\subseteq\!I_{d+j},\ \forall\ d\geq 1\}

Below we often abbreviate
End_{X}\!:=\!End_{\Bbbk}(R)
and
End^{(j)}_{X}\!:=\!End^{(j)}_{\Bbbk}(R).

•

Der^{(j)}_{X}:=\{\xi\in Der_{X}|\ \xi(I_{d})\subseteq I_{j+d},\ \forall\ d\geq 1\}=Der_{X}\cap End^{(j)}_{\Bbbk}(R).

We get the filtration by
R
-submodules,
Der_{X}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq Der^{(-1)}_{X}\supseteq Der^{(0)}_{X}\supseteq Der^{(1)}_{X}\supset\cdots
.

E.g. for the filtration by powers of ideals,
I^{\bullet}\subset R
, we have
Der_{X}=Der^{(-1)}_{X}
and

Der^{(j)}_{X}\supseteq I^{j+1}\cdot Der_{X}
.

2.5.
The maps
exp,ln:Der_{X}\leftrightarrows Aut_{X}
and their approximations. Vector field integration

Let
(R,{\mathfrak{m}},I^{\bullet})
be a local filtered
\Bbbk
-algebra of §
2.1
.ii. Assume
\Bbbk\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
and
R
is complete with respect to the filtration.
Then the derivations and automorphisms of
R
admit the exponential and logarithmic maps, see Theorem 3.6 of
[
B.G.K.22
]
:

(9)

\forall\ j\geq 1:\quad\quad Der^{(j)}_{X}\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{\to}\limits^{exp}}$}\ Aut^{(j)}_{X},\quad exp(\xi):=e^{\xi}=\sum\nolimits^{\infty}_{i=0}\frac{\xi^{i}}{i!},

\forall\ j\geq 1:\quad\quad Aut^{(j)}_{X}\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{\to}\limits^{ln}}$}\ Der^{(j)}_{X},\quad ln(\Phi):=-\sum\nolimits^{\infty}_{i=1}\frac{({1\hskip-2.84544pt\rm I}-\Phi)^{i}}{i}.

When
\Bbbk\not\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
or the ring
R
is not
I^{\bullet}
-complete, one cannot use the classical exponential/logarithmic maps. But in many cases
one can use the following “
N
’th jet
approximations” to these maps. Fix
1\leq N\leq\infty.

For our filtration criteria, §
4.3
, §
5.3
, §
7.4
,
we impose the conditions (for all
j\geq 1
):

\bullet

\boldsymbol{jet_{N}(Exp)\!:}
every derivation
\xi\!\in\!Der^{(j)}_{X}
defines an
automorphism
\!jet_{N}(e^{\xi})\!:=\!\sum^{N}_{i=0}\!\!\frac{\xi^{i}}{i!}\!+\!\phi_{\xi}\!\!\in\!Aut^{(j)}_{X}\!.

(Here
\phi_{\xi}\in End^{(jN+1)}_{X}
is an auxiliary
\Bbbk
-linear endomorphism, see §
2.4
.iii.)

\bullet

\!\!\boldsymbol{jet_{N}(Ln)\!:}
every automorphism
\!\phi\!\in\!Aut^{(j)}_{X}
defines a
derivation
\!jet_{N}(ln(\phi))\!:=\!-\!\sum^{N}_{i=1}\!\frac{({1\hskip-2.84544pt\rm I}-\phi)^{i}}{i}\!+\!\xi_{\phi}\!\!\in\!\!Der^{(j)}_{X}\!.

(Here
\xi_{\phi}\in End^{(jN+1)}_{X}
is an auxiliary
\Bbbk
-linear endomorphism.)

These conditions imply, in particular,
2,\dots,N\in\Bbbk^{\times}
, see §
2.1
.i. Thus
char(\Bbbk)>N
or
char(\Bbbk)=0.

For our “
\mathscr{G}
-implicit function theorems”, §
4.1
,§
5.1
,§
7.2
, we impose a variation of condition
jet_{1}
.
Take a derivation
\xi\!\in\!Der_{X}
with
\xi({\mathfrak{m}})\subseteq{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}.

If ​
J\!=\!0
​ then the map
f(x)\!\to\!f(x\!+\xi(x)),
​ defines an automorphism
\Phi\!\in\!Aut_{X}.
​
If
J\!\neq\!0
then
f(x)\!\to\!f(x\!+\xi(x))
(defined on the representatives)
is not a well-defined self-map of
R,
as it does not preserve ​
J.
Therefore we impose the condition:

•

\boldsymbol{jet_{0}:}
Any map
x\to x+\xi(x)
, with
\xi\in Der_{X}
and
\xi({\mathfrak{m}})\subseteq\mathfrak{a}^{2}\cdot{\mathfrak{m}}^{j}
(for some
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq{\mathfrak{m}}
and
j\geq 0
),
extends to an automorphism
\Phi\in Aut_{X}
that satisfies:
\Phi(x)-x-\xi(x)\in\mathfrak{a}^{3}\cdot{\mathfrak{m}}^{j}
.
Moreover, presenting
\Phi=Id+\xi+\varphi_{\xi}
, one has (for
c_{1},c_{2}\in\Bbbk
):
\varphi_{c_{1}\xi_{1}+c_{2}\xi_{2}}
is a power series in
c_{1},c_{2}
.

Example 2.1
.

​​
i.​

The conditions
jet_{N}(Exp),jet_{N}(Ln)
hold, e.g. in the following cases.

•

R={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk[[x]]$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
, with
\Bbbk\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
, and any
0\leq N\leq\infty
. Here one takes just the full power series in (
9
).

•

R={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
or
R={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
, with
\Bbbk\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
, and any
0\leq N<\infty
. Here one shows that such
\phi_{\xi}
,
\xi_{\phi}
do exists,
under very weak assumptions on the filtration:
Der^{(1)}_{X}({\mathfrak{m}})\subseteq{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}.

For more detail see
[
B.G.K.22
, Theorem 3.6]
.

•

R=\Bbbk[[x]]
,
\Bbbk\{x\}
,
\Bbbk\langle x\rangle
, (and any
\Bbbk
), with
2,\dots,N\in\Bbbk^{\times}
or
N=0
,
see §3.2.2 of
[
B.G.K.22
]
.

​​
ii.​

The condition
jet_{N}(Exp)
(resp.
jet_{N}(Ln)
) obviously implies
jet_{l}(Exp)
(resp.
jet_{l}(Ln)
) for
l<N
.

​​
iii.​

The condition
jet_{\infty}(Exp)
means: every vector field
\xi\in T_{X}
integrates to the flow
\Phi_{t}\circlearrowright X
.

​​
iv.​

For
char(\Bbbk)\!>0
and
J\!\neq\!0
already the condition
jet_{1}
is non-trivial, see §3.2 of
[
B.G.K.22
]
.
For example, let
\Bbbk
be a field of characteristic
p
and take
f(x,y)=x^{p}+y^{3p}\in\Bbbk[[x,y]]
.
Take the ring
R={\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk[[x,y]]$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$(f)$}\right.}
filtered by the ideals
(x,y)^{\bullet}
.
Take
\xi:=y^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\in Der^{(1)}_{X}
. The conditions
jet_{0}
and
jet_{1}(Exp)
imply: there exist
\phi_{x},\phi_{y}\in(x,y)^{3}
such that
the map
(x,y)\to(x+y^{2}+\phi_{x},y+\phi_{y})
defines an automorphism of
R
. But then we must have

(10)

f(x,y)\to f(x+y^{2}+\phi_{x},y+\phi_{y})=x^{p}+y^{2p}+(\phi_{x})^{p}+y^{3p}+(\phi_{y})^{3p}\in(f(x,y))=(x^{p}+y^{3p}).

And this condition is clearly non-resolvable because of the term
y^{2p}
.

3.
Group actions
\mathscr{G}\!\circlearrowright\!Maps(X,\!Y)
and the corresponding tangent spaces
T_{\mathscr{G}}f

Classically one studies maps of germs
(\Bbbk^{n},o)\to(\Bbbk^{p},o)
, for
\Bbbk=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
, up to the right (
\mathscr{R}
), left (
\mathscr{L}
),
contact (
{\mathscr{K}}\!
) or left-right (
\mathscr{A}
)
equivalences. We set up the corresponding general notions.

Let
X
and ​
Y\!=\!(\Bbbk^{p},o)
be the formal/
\Bbbk
-analytic/
\Bbbk
-Nash germs, i.e.
(R_{X},\!R_{Y})
is one of the pairs,

({\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk[[x]]$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},\Bbbk[[y]]),({\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},\Bbbk\{y\}),({\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},\Bbbk\langle y\rangle),
see §
2.1
.ii.

3.1.
The basic group-actions of Singularity Theory,
\mathbf{\mathscr{R},\mathscr{L},\mathscr{C},{\mathscr{K}}\!,\mathscr{A}\circlearrowright Maps(X,\!Y)}

​i.​

The elements of
R_{X}
are presentable by power series in
x.

Therefore the “coordinate changes”,
Aut_{X}\!\circlearrowright\!X,
induce the action
Aut_{X}\!\circlearrowright\!Maps(X,\!Y)\!=\!{\mathfrak{m}}\!\cdot\!R^{\oplus p}_{X}
by composition on the right,

[
Mon. Nuñ.-Bal.
, §3.1]

(11)

\Phi_{X}(f)=\Phi(f_{1},\dots,f_{p})=f\circ\Phi^{-1}_{X}=(f_{1}\circ\Phi^{-1}_{X},\dots,f_{p}\circ\Phi^{-1}_{X}),\hskip 56.9055ptf\in{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

Traditionally one denotes this group action by
\mathscr{R}:=Aut_{X}.

The action
\mathscr{R}\circlearrowright{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
is
\Bbbk
-linear.

​ii.​

Present the automorphisms
Aut_{Y}\circlearrowright R_{Y}
explicitly,
(y_{1},\dots,y_{p})=y\to\Phi_{Y}(y)=\big(\Phi_{1}(y),\dots,\Phi_{p}(y)\big)
.
Accordingly define the left action
Aut_{Y}\circlearrowright Maps(X,\!Y)={\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
, by
f\to\Phi_{Y}(y)|_{f}:=(\Phi_{1}(f),\dots,\Phi_{p}(f)).

See
[
Mon. Nuñ.-Bal.
, §3.2]
. Traditionally one denotes this group action by
\mathscr{L}:=Aut_{Y}.

Note that the group
\mathscr{L}
does not act on the whole module
R^{\oplus p}_{X}
,
as the composition
\Phi_{Y}(y)|_{f}
is not defined for
f\not\in{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
.
(See §
2.1
.iv.)

The action
Aut_{Y}\circlearrowright R_{Y}
is
\Bbbk
-linear, but the action
\mathscr{L}\circlearrowright{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
is neither additive nor multiplicative.

Occasionally we write also
Aut_{X},Aut_{Y}\circlearrowright Maps(X,\!Y).

​iii.​

The contact transformations
X\times Y\to X\times Y
are defined by
(x,y)\to(\Phi(x),\Psi(x,y))
, see e.g.
[
Mon. Nuñ.-Bal.
, pg. 108]
.
Here

\Phi\in Aut_{X}
and

\Psi\in Maps(X\times Y,Y)
with
\Psi(o,-)\in Aut_{Y}
. Algebraically, in the formal case:

\Psi({\mathfrak{m}}_{X},{\mathfrak{m}}_{Y})\in{\mathfrak{m}}_{Y}\cdot R_{X}[[y]]^{\oplus p}
, with
\Psi(o,{\mathfrak{m}}_{Y})\in\Bbbk[[y]]^{\oplus p}.
(And similarly in the
\Bbbk
-analytic/
\Bbbk
-Nash cases.)

Accordingly we define the contact group action
{\mathscr{K}}\!\circlearrowright Maps(X,\!Y)={\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
by
f(x)\to\Psi(x,f(\Phi^{-1}(x))
.

Taking
\Phi\!=\!Id_{X}\in Aut_{X}
we get the subgroup
\mathscr{C}\subset{\mathscr{K}}\!
. It acts on the fibres of the projection
X\!\times\!Y\!\to\!X.

This presents the contact group as the semi-direct product
{\mathscr{K}}\!\!=\!\mathscr{C}\!\rtimes\!\mathscr{R},
see pg. 156 of
[
A.G.L.V.
]
or page 109 of
[
Mon. Nuñ.-Bal.
]
.

The action
{\mathscr{K}}\!\circlearrowright Maps(X,\!Y)={\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
is neither additive nor
\Bbbk
-multiplicative.
But the
{\mathscr{K}}\!
-orbits coincide with the orbits of the much smaller group
{\mathscr{K}}\!^{lin}:=GL(p,R_{X})\rtimes Aut_{X}\circlearrowright{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

For
R\!=\!\Bbbk\{x\}
,
\Bbbk\in\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
this is well known,
see e.g.
[
Mon. Nuñ.-Bal.
, pg.110]
. For the general case see
[
B.K.16
, pg.123]
.
This action
{\mathscr{K}}\!^{lin}\circlearrowright Maps(X,\!Y)
is
\Bbbk
-linear.

Two maps
f,{\tilde{f}}\in{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
are
{\mathscr{K}}\!^{lin}
-equivalent iff the corresponding local
\Bbbk
-algebras are isomorphic,

{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$R_{X}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$(f)$}\right.}\cong{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$R_{X}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$({\tilde{f}})$}\right.}.
And the later is an isomorphism of scheme-germs,
Spec({\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$R_{X}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$(f)$}\right.})\cong Spec({\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$R_{X}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$({\tilde{f}})$}\right.}).

Therefore the
{\mathscr{K}}\!^{lin}
,
{\mathscr{K}}\!
-equivalences coincide with the
V
-equivalence of
[
Martinet.76
]
.

​iv.​

The action of the left-right group,

\mathscr{A}:=\mathscr{L}\times\mathscr{R}\circlearrowright Maps(X,\!Y)={\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
, is defined by

(12)

(\Phi_{Y},\Phi_{X})(f):=\Phi_{Y}\circ f\circ\Phi_{X}^{-1}:=\Phi_{Y}(y)|_{f\circ\Phi_{X}^{-1}}.

This action is compatible with the product structure:

(\Phi_{Y},\Phi_{X})\circ(\tilde{\Phi}_{Y},\tilde{\Phi}_{X})=(\Phi_{Y}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{Y},\Phi_{X}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{X})
. In particular,

(\Phi_{Y},\Phi_{X})=(\Phi_{Y},Id_{X})\circ(Id_{Y},\Phi_{X})=(Id_{Y},\Phi_{X})\circ(\Phi_{Y},Id_{X})
.

While the actions
\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!^{lin}\circlearrowright{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
are
\Bbbk
-linear,
the actions
\mathscr{L},\mathscr{A}\circlearrowright{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
are neither additive nor
\Bbbk
-multiplicative.
To my knowledge it is not known whether/how the
\mathscr{A}
-action can be “reduced”
to a
\Bbbk
-linear one. See
[
Damon.91
]
,
[
Mond-Montaldi.91
]
,
[
Houston-Wik Atique.13
]
for the related works.

3.2.
The (extended) tangent spaces
\mathbf{T_{\mathscr{R}},T_{\mathscr{L}},T_{\mathscr{A}},T_{\mathscr{K}}\!}

The natural (extended) tangent spaces to the groups
\mathscr{R},\mathscr{L},{\mathscr{K}}\!,\mathscr{A}
are defined via the modules of derivations
and endomorphisms, see §
2.4
and
[
Mon. Nuñ.-Bal.
]
.

(13)

T_{\mathscr{R}}:=Der_{X},\quad\quad\quad T_{\mathscr{L}}:=Der_{Y},\quad\quad\quad T_{\mathscr{K}}\!:=End_{R_{X}}(R^{\oplus p}_{X})\oplus T_{\mathscr{R}},\quad\quad\quad T_{\mathscr{A}}:=T_{\mathscr{L}}\oplus T_{\mathscr{R}}.

As
R_{Y}\!=\!\Bbbk[[y]],\Bbbk\{y\},\Bbbk\langle y\rangle,
​ one can write explicitly
Der_{Y}\!=\!R_{Y}\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{p}}\rangle,
​ i.e.
​
\xi_{Y}\!=\!\sum\xi_{Y,i}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}},
​ for

\xi_{Y,i}\!\in\!R_{Y}.

The name “tangent space” is justified by the relation
T_{\mathscr{G}}\rightleftarrows\mathscr{G}
, see §
2.5
, and
[
B.G.K.22
, §3]
.

These tangent spaces act on the (extended) space of maps as in the classical case:

(14)

T_{\mathscr{R}}\circlearrowright R^{\oplus p}_{X}\quad\quad\quad{\rm by}\quad\quad\quad\xi_{X}(f):=\xi_{X}(f_{1},\dots,f_{p})=(\xi_{X}(f_{1}),\dots,\xi_{X}(f_{1}))\in R^{\oplus p}_{X},

T_{\mathscr{L}}\!\!:\ {\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}\to R^{\oplus p}_{X}\quad\quad\quad{\rm by}\quad\quad\quad\xi_{Y}(f):=\xi_{Y}(y)|_{f}=(\xi_{Y,1}|_{f},\dots,\xi_{Y,p}|_{f})\in R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

The action
T_{\mathscr{R}}\circlearrowright R^{\oplus p}_{X}
is
\Bbbk
-linear. This embeds
T_{\mathscr{R}}=Der_{X}\subset End_{\Bbbk}(R^{\oplus p}_{X})
as an
R_{X}
-submodule.
The map
T_{\mathscr{L}}:{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}\to R^{\oplus p}_{X}
is by power series, it is neither additive nor
\Bbbk
-multiplicative.

Given a map
f\in{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X},
one gets the (extended) image tangent space

T_{\mathscr{G}}f\subseteq R^{\oplus p}_{X}
. For our groups:

(15)

T_{\mathscr{R}}f=Der_{X}(f),\quad\quad T_{\mathscr{L}}f=f^{\#}(R_{Y})^{\oplus p},\quad\quad T_{\mathscr{A}}f=T_{\mathscr{R}}f+T_{\mathscr{L}}f,\quad\quad T_{\mathscr{K}}\!f=T_{\mathscr{R}}f+(f)\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

Remark 3.1
.

i.

Classically these
T_{\mathscr{G}}f
are called “the extended tangent spaces”, with the notations from differential geometry,
[
Mon. Nuñ.-Bal.
, pg.72]
:

(16)

R_{X}^{\oplus p}=\theta(f),\quad\quad\quad T_{\mathscr{R}^{e}}f=tf(\theta_{n}),\quad\quad\quad T_{\mathscr{L}^{e}}f=\omega f(\theta_{p}),\quad\quad\quad T_{{\mathscr{K}}\!^{e}}f=tf(\theta_{n})+f^{*}{\mathfrak{m}}_{p}\cdot\theta(f).

We avoid the notation
T_{\mathscr{G}^{e}}
to prevent any confusion with the filtered tangent spaces,
T_{\mathscr{G}^{(j)}},
§
3.3
.

ii.

In zero characteristic,
\Bbbk\supseteq\mathbb{Q},
these “image tangent spaces” coincide with the
“tangent spaces to the orbit”,
T_{\mathscr{G}}f=T_{\mathscr{G}f}
. In positive characteristic the two notions differ, see §1.5.2 of
[
B.G.K.22
]
.

iii.

Note that
T_{\mathscr{R}}f,T_{\mathscr{K}}\!f\subset R^{\oplus p}_{X}
are
R_{X}
-submodules, while
T_{\mathscr{L}}f,T_{\mathscr{A}}f\subset R^{\oplus p}_{X}

are only
R_{Y}
-submodules.
Here
R_{Y}
acts by substitution,

q(y_{1},\dots,y_{p})\cdot T_{\mathscr{A}}f:=q(f_{1},\dots,f_{p})\cdot T_{\mathscr{A}}f\subset R^{\oplus p}_{X}
, see (
3
).

iv.

The actions
\Phi_{X},\!\Phi_{Y}\!\circlearrowright\!Maps(X,\!Y)
commute, see (
12
). The derivations do not commute:

(17)

\xi_{X}(\xi_{Y}(y)|_{f})=\xi_{X}(\xi_{Y,1}|_{f},\dots,\xi_{Y,p}|_{f})=(\sum\nolimits_{i}\partial_{y_{i}}\xi_{Y,1}|_{f}\cdot\xi_{X}(f_{i}),\dots,\sum\nolimits_{i}\partial_{y_{i}}\xi_{Y,p}|_{f}\cdot\xi_{X}(f_{i}))=\\
=\sum\nolimits_{i}\partial_{y_{i}}\xi_{Y}(y)|_{f}\cdot\xi_{X}(f_{i})\neq\xi_{Y}(y)|_{\xi_{X}(f)}.

To pass from
\xi_{X}\cdot\xi_{Y}
to
\xi_{Y}\cdot\xi_{X}
we aply lemma
3.9
. This is used in §
7.4
.

3.3.
Filtrations on the group
\mathscr{G}
and on the tangent space
T_{\mathscr{G}}

Fix an ideal
I\subseteq{\mathfrak{m}}\subset R_{X}
and take the filtration by powers of this ideal,
M_{\bullet}:=I^{\bullet}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}\subseteq R^{\oplus p}_{X}
.
Thus
M_{\bullet}\subset Maps(X,\!Y)
consists of maps that ‘vanish to a given order” on the locus
V(I)\subset X
.

3.3.1.

For
\mathscr{G}=\mathscr{R}
or
\mathscr{L}
we get the corresponding filtrations on the groups/tangent spaces, as in §
2.4
.iii:

(18)

\mathscr{G}^{(j)}:=\{g\in\mathscr{G}|\ g\cdot M_{d}=M_{d}\ \text{ and }\ g|_{{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$M_{d}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$M_{d+j}$}\right.}}=Id|_{{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$M_{d}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$M_{d+j}$}\right.}},\ \forall d\geq 1\}.

T_{\mathscr{G}^{(j)}}:=\{\xi\in T_{\mathscr{G}}|\quad\xi(M_{d})\subseteq M_{d+j},\quad\forall d\geq 1\}.

For the right equivalence we have
T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}=T_{\mathscr{R}}\cap End^{(j)}_{\Bbbk}(R^{\oplus p}_{X})
and
T_{\mathscr{R}}=T_{\mathscr{R}^{(-1)}}\supsetneq T_{\mathscr{R}^{(0)}}
,
cf. §
2.4
.

For the left equivalence we have

\mathscr{L}^{(j)}=\{\Phi_{Y}\in\mathscr{L}|\ \Phi_{Y}(y)-y|_{I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}}\in I^{d+j}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}\}
,
i.e.
\Phi_{Y}(y)=y+\phi(y)
, where
\phi(I^{d})\subseteq I^{d+j}
.
In particular
\mathscr{L}^{(0)}=\mathscr{L}
.

The tangent space filtration is
T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}=\{\xi_{Y}\in Der_{Y}|\ \xi_{Y}(y)|_{I^{d}}\subseteq I^{d+j}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X},\ \forall\ d\geq 1\}
.
In particular:

(19)

T_{\mathscr{L}}\supsetneq T_{\mathscr{L}^{(0)}}=(y)\cdot T_{\mathscr{L}}\supsetneq T_{\mathscr{L}^{(1)}}=(y)^{2}\cdot T_{\mathscr{L}}.

More generally,
T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}=\mathfrak{b}_{j}\cdot T_{\mathscr{L}}
, for the largest ideal
\mathfrak{b}_{j}\subset R_{Y}
satisfying:
f^{\#}(\mathfrak{b}_{j})\subseteq I^{d+j}
for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
all
f\in I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
. In particular, the tangent image is
T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}f=f^{\#}(\mathfrak{b}_{j}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{Y})\subseteq I^{d+j}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
.

We have the obvious inclusions:
(y)^{j+1}\subseteq\mathfrak{b}_{j}\subset R_{Y}
, and
(f)^{j+1}\subseteq f^{\#}(\mathfrak{b}_{j})\cdot R_{X}
when
(f)\subseteq I
.
These are equalities when
\Bbbk\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
is a field, but can be proper if
\Bbbk
is a finite field or a ring.

Example 3.2
.

i.

Suppose
R_{X}
is one of
\Bbbk[[x]]
,
\Bbbk\{x\}
,
\Bbbk\langle x\rangle
, where
\Bbbk
is an infinite field. Suppose
I={\mathfrak{m}}=(x)\subset R_{X}
. Take a map
f\in(x)\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
.
Then:

T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}=(x)^{j+1}\cdot T_{\mathscr{R}},\quad\quad T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}f=(x)^{j+1}\cdot Der_{X}(f),\quad\quad T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}=(y)^{j+1}\cdot T_{\mathscr{L}},\quad\quad T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}f=f^{\#}(y)^{j+1}\cdot T_{\mathscr{L}}f\subset R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

In the classical notations, cf. (
16
):
T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}f=tf({\mathfrak{m}}^{j+1}_{n}\cdot\Theta_{n})

and
T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}f=\omega f({\mathfrak{m}}^{j+1}_{p}\cdot\Theta_{p})
.

ii.

Consider
Maps((\Bbbk,o),(\Bbbk^{2},o))
, where
\Bbbk
is a field with two elements, and the rings are complete. Take the filtration
(x)^{\bullet}\cdot R^{\oplus 2}_{X}
.
Take the derivation
\xi_{Y}=q(y)\partial_{y_{1}}+0\cdot\partial_{y_{2}}\in Der_{Y}
, where
q(y)=y_{1}y_{2}(y_{1}+y_{2})\in\Bbbk[[y_{1},y_{2}]]
.
Then
q((x)^{i},(x)^{i})\subseteq(x)^{3i+1}
. Thus
\xi_{Y}\in T_{\mathscr{L}^{(3)}}
, even though
q(y)\not\in(y)^{4}\subset\Bbbk[[y_{1},y_{2}]].

iii.

Let
\Bbbk=\tilde{\Bbbk}[[t]]
for a field
\tilde{\Bbbk}\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
. Take
I=(t,{\mathfrak{m}})
, then
T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}=(t,y)^{j+1}T_{\mathscr{L}}\supsetneq(y)^{j+1}T_{\mathscr{L}}
.

Remark 3.3
.

i.

In the definition (
18
) it is important to assume
d\geq 1
, rather than
d\geq 0
.
In fact the group
\mathscr{L}
does not act on the whole
R^{\oplus p}_{X}
, thus the condition “
g|_{{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$M_{0}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$M_{j}$}\right.}}=Id|_{{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$M_{0}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$M_{j}$}\right.}}
” makes no sense.
One could restrict to
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{${\mathfrak{m}}\cdot M_{0}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$M_{j}$}\right.}
, but then, for
\sqrt{I}\subsetneq{\mathfrak{m}}
, the condition

g|_{{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{${\mathfrak{m}}\cdot M_{0}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$M_{j}$}\right.}}=Id|_{{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{${\mathfrak{m}}\cdot M_{0}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$M_{j}$}\right.}}
would imply
\mathscr{L}^{(j)}=\{e\}
. Similarly, the condition

\xi({\mathfrak{m}}\cdot M_{0})\subseteq M_{j}
would imply
T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}=(0)
.

ii.

The relation
(y)\cdot T_{\mathscr{L}}=T_{\mathscr{L}^{(0)}}
motivates the notation
T_{\mathscr{L}^{(-1)}}:=T_{\mathscr{L}}
.
This should be used with caution, as
T_{\mathscr{L}}(I^{d})\not\subseteq I
for any
d
.

3.3.2.

For the groups
\mathscr{A},{\mathscr{K}}\!
we define the filtration in a special way:

•

\mathscr{A}^{(j)}:=\mathscr{L}^{(j)}\times\mathscr{R}^{(j)}
and
T_{\mathscr{A}^{(j)}}:=T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}\oplus T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}
.

•

{\mathscr{K}}\!^{(j)}:=\mathscr{C}^{(j)}\rtimes\mathscr{R}^{(j)}
and
T_{{\mathscr{K}}\!^{(j)}}:=T_{\mathscr{C}^{(j)}}\oplus T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}
. Here

T_{\mathscr{C}^{(j)}}:=Mat_{p\times p}(I^{j})
.

In particular
T_{\mathscr{K}}\!=T_{{\mathscr{K}}\!^{(-1)}}
. As in remark
3.3
.ii we denote
T_{\mathscr{A}^{(-1)}}:=T_{\mathscr{A}}
. Again care is needed, as

T_{\mathscr{A}}(I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X})\not\subseteq I\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
for any
d
.

One would like to use the general definition (
18
), for the groups
\mathscr{A},{\mathscr{K}}\!
, i.e. to define

(20)

\mathscr{A}^{(j)}=(\mathscr{L}\times\mathscr{R})^{(j)}\quad\text{ and }\quad T_{\mathscr{A}^{(j)}}=(T_{\mathscr{R}}\oplus T_{\mathscr{L}})^{(j)},\quad\text{ and so on.}

In this definition
\mathscr{A}^{(j)},T_{\mathscr{A}^{(j)}}
become non-explicit/complicated.
But the two versions often coincide.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
j\geq 1
, and
\Bbbk
is an infinite field, and
I^{\bullet}\neq(0)
for each
\bullet\in\mathbb{N}
. Then:

1.

(\mathscr{L}\times\mathscr{R})^{(j)}=\mathscr{L}^{(j)}\times\mathscr{R}^{(j)}
, i.e.
(Aut_{Y}\times Aut_{X})^{(j)}=Aut^{(j)}_{Y}\times Aut^{(j)}_{X}
.

2.

(T_{\mathscr{L}}\oplus T_{\mathscr{R}})^{(j)}=T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}\oplus T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}
, i.e.

(Der_{Y}\oplus Der_{X})^{(j)}=Der^{(j)}_{Y}\oplus Der^{(j)}_{X}
.

Proof.
The inclusions
(\mathscr{L}\times\mathscr{R})^{(j)}\geq\mathscr{L}^{(j)}\times\mathscr{R}^{(j)}
and
(T_{\mathscr{L}}\oplus T_{\mathscr{R}})^{(j)}\supseteq T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}\oplus T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}
are
obvious. We prove the parts
\leq
and
\subseteq
.

Below we use the property: there exists
f\in I
such that
f^{d}\in I^{d}\setminus I^{d+1}
for each
d\geq 1
. Indeed, otherwise we
would get
I^{d}\subseteq I^{d+1}
for
d\gg 1
, as
I
is finitely generated. But then
I^{d}=0
by Nakayama.

1.

Suppose
(\Phi_{Y},\Phi_{X})\in(\mathscr{L}\times\mathscr{R})^{(j)}
. As
\Phi_{Y}\in\mathscr{L}=\mathscr{L}^{(0)}
, we get:
\Phi_{X}\in\mathscr{R}^{(0)}
.
Suppose
\Phi_{Y}\in\mathscr{L}^{(l)}\setminus\mathscr{L}^{(l+1)}
, then, after a
\Bbbk
-linear reshuffling of
y
-coordinates, we can assume:

\Phi_{Y}(y_{1},0,\dots,0)=(q(y_{1}),\dots).
Here:

(21)

q(y_{1})=y_{1}+a\cdot y^{{\tilde{l}}+1}_{1}+h.o.t.\quad\text{(for some ${\tilde{l}}\leq l$)},\quad a\in\Bbbk^{\times},\quad h.o.t.\in(y_{1})^{l+2}\quad\text{and}\quad a\cdot I^{{\tilde{l}}+1}\subseteq I^{l+1}.

As
I^{\bullet}\neq 0
for each
\bullet,
we get
{\tilde{l}}=l.

For each
f\in I
define the difference
\Delta_{f}:=[\Phi_{Y}(y_{1},0,\dots,0)|_{f}]_{1}-\Phi_{X}(f)=q(f)-\Phi_{X}(f).

By our assumption
\Delta_{f\circ\Phi^{-1}_{X}}\in I^{ord(f)+j}.
As
\Phi_{X}\in\mathscr{R}^{(0)},
we get:
\Delta_{f}\in I^{ord(f)+j}.

•

The case
l=0
. Then
\Phi_{Y}\in\mathscr{L}^{(0)}\setminus\mathscr{L}^{(1)}
and
\Phi_{X}\in\mathscr{R}^{(0)}\setminus\mathscr{R}^{(1)}.

For each
f\in I
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
we get

(22)

I^{d\cdot ord(f)+j}\ni f^{d-1}\cdot\Delta_{f}-\Delta_{f^{d}}=f^{d-1}\cdot\Phi_{X}(f)-\Phi_{X}(f)^{d}+(h.o.t.),\quad\text{where}\quad(h.o.t)\in I^{(d+1)\cdot ord(f)}.

As this holds for each
f\in I,
and the field
\Bbbk
is infinite, we get:
\Phi_{X}\in\mathscr{R}^{(1)},
i.e. a contradiction.

•

The case
l\geq 1
. For any
f\in I
,
c\in\Bbbk^{\times}
we have:

(23)

\Delta_{c\cdot f}-c\cdot\Delta_{f}=q(c\cdot f)-c\cdot q(f)=a\cdot(c^{l+1}-c)\cdot f^{l+1}+h.o.t.\in I^{ord(f)+j}.

As
\Bbbk
is infinite there exists
c\in\Bbbk^{\times}
for which
c^{l+1}-c\in\Bbbk^{\times}.
Thus for each
f\in I
and
l>0
we get:
f^{l+1}\in I^{ord(f)+j}
.
Therefore
l\geq j
, hence
\Phi_{Y}\in\mathscr{L}^{(j)}
, and thus
\Phi_{X}\in\mathscr{R}^{(j)}
.

2.

Suppose
(\xi_{Y},\xi_{X})\in(T_{\mathscr{L}}\oplus T_{\mathscr{R}})^{(j)}
, thus
(\xi_{Y},\xi_{X})(I\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X})\subseteq I^{1+j}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
.
If
\xi_{Y}\in T_{\mathscr{L}}\setminus T_{\mathscr{L}^{(0)}}
then
\xi_{Y}(I)\not\subseteq{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Then for
f\in(I\cap{\mathfrak{m}}^{2})\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
we get:
(\xi_{Y},\xi_{X})(f)\not\in{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

Therefore necessarily
\xi_{Y}\in T_{\mathscr{L}^{(0)}},
and then
\xi_{X}\in T_{\mathscr{R}^{(0)}}
.

Suppose
\xi_{Y}\in T_{\mathscr{L}^{(l)}}\setminus T_{\mathscr{L}^{(l+1)}}
, then after a
\Bbbk
-linear reshuffling of
y
-coordinates we can assume
\xi_{Y}(y_{1},0,\dots,0)=(q(y_{1}),\dots).

Here
q(y_{1})=a\cdot y^{{\tilde{l}}+1}_{1}+h.o.t.
(for some
{\tilde{l}}\leq l
),
a\in\Bbbk^{\times},

h.o.t.\in(y_{1})^{l+2}
and
a\cdot I^{{\tilde{l}}+1}\subseteq I^{l+1}.

Therefore
{\tilde{l}}=l.

•

The case
l=0
. For any
f\in I
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(24)

[(\xi_{Y},\xi_{X})|_{f^{d},0,\dots,0}]_{1}-d\cdot f^{d-1}\cdot[(\xi_{Y},\xi_{X})|_{f,0,\dots,0}]_{1}=q(f^{d})-d\cdot f^{d-1}\cdot q(f)=a(1-d)f^{d}+h.o.t\in I^{ord(f^{d})+j}.

Choose
f\in I\setminus I^{2}
satisfying
f^{2}\in I^{2}\setminus I^{3}
to get the contradiction.

•

The case
l\geq 1
. For any
f\in I
and
c\in\Bbbk
we have

(25)

[(\xi_{Y},\xi_{X})|_{c\cdot f,0,\dots,0}]_{1}-c\cdot[(\xi_{Y},\xi_{X})|_{f,0,\dots,0}]_{1}=q(c\cdot f)-c\cdot q(f)=a(c^{l+1}-c)f^{l+1}+h.o.t\in I^{ord(f)+j}.

As
\Bbbk
is infinite there exists
c\in\Bbbk
such that
c^{l+1}-c\neq 0
. Therefore we get
l\geq j
, hence
\xi_{Y}\in T_{\mathscr{L}^{(j)}}
,
and thus
\xi_{X}\in T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}
.
\blacksquare

Remark 3.5
.

​​By similar arguments one ​can prove, for the contact group
{\mathscr{K}}\!\!=\!\mathscr{C}\!\rtimes\!\mathscr{R},
and ​an ​infinite field
\Bbbk:

(\mathscr{C}\rtimes\mathscr{R})^{(j)}=\mathscr{C}^{(j)}\rtimes\mathscr{R}^{(j)},\quad(GL(p,R_{X})\rtimes Aut_{X})^{(j)}=GL^{(j)}(p,R_{X})\rtimes Aut^{(j)}_{X},\quad(T_{\mathscr{C}}\oplus T_{\mathscr{R}})^{(j)}=T_{\mathscr{C}^{(j)}}\oplus T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}.

3.4.
The closures in the filtration topology

For our groups
\mathscr{G}=\mathscr{R},{\mathscr{K}}\!,\mathscr{A}
of §
3.1
we have the closures of the orbits in the filtration topology (for
M_{\bullet}=I^{\bullet}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
):

(26)

\overline{T_{\mathscr{G}^{(j)}}f}=\cap_{\bullet\geq 1}(T_{\mathscr{G}^{(j)}}f+M_{\bullet}),\quad\quad\quad\quad\overline{\mathscr{G}^{(j)}f}=\cap_{\bullet\geq 1}(\mathscr{G}^{(j)}f+M_{\bullet}).

Lemma 3.6
.

​1.​​

Any
R_{X}
-submodule of
R^{\oplus p}_{X}
is closed in
I^{\bullet}
-topology.

In particular, the tangent spaces
T_{\mathscr{R}^{(j)}}f
,
T_{{\mathscr{K}}\!^{(j)}}f\subseteq R^{\oplus p}_{X}
are closed.

​2.​​

The orbits
\mathscr{R}^{(j)}f
,
{\mathscr{K}}\!^{(j)}f
are closed.

Proof.

​1.​​

Let
M\subseteq R^{\oplus p}_{X}
then
M+I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}\supseteq\overline{M}
for any
d\geq 1
. Therefore
M+(I^{d}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X})\cap\overline{M}\supseteq\overline{M}
.
Applying the Artin-Rees lemma to the (finitely generated, §
2.1
.viii) submodule
\overline{M}\subset R^{\oplus p}_{X}

we get:
M+I\cdot\overline{M}\supseteq\overline{M}
. Then Nakayama gives
M=\overline{M}
.

​2.​​

(The
{\mathscr{K}}\!
-case.) Suppose
f+g\in\overline{{\mathscr{K}}\!^{(j)}f}
for a perturbation
g\in{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}.

Thus
f+g\in\overline{({\mathscr{K}}\!^{lin})^{(j)}f}.

Thus the system of implicit
function equations
f(\Phi(x))=U\cdot(f+g)
has an order-by-order solution.
By the theorem of Pfister-Popescu, §
2.3
.v., it has a formal solutions,

(\hat{\Phi},\hat{U})\in\widehat{({\mathscr{K}}\!^{lin})^{(j)}}
.

For the rings
{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\{x\}$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.},{\footnotesize\left.\raisebox{1.37775pt}{$\Bbbk\langle x\rangle$}\!/\!\raisebox{-1.37775pt}{$J$}\right.}
apply the Artin approximation to
get an ordinary solution,
(\Phi,{U})\!\in\!({\mathscr{K}}\!^{lin})^{(j)}.

\blacksquare

Remark 3.7
.

i.

Part 1 is a pure commutative algebra, it holds for Noetherian local rings, provided all the objects are defined.

ii.

Below we use the simple observation: to verify the inclusion
\overline{\mathscr{G}^{(j)}f}\supseteq\{f\}+M_{d}

it is enough to verify
\mathscr{G}^{(j)}f+M_{\bullet+1}\supseteq\{f\}+M_{\bullet}
for every
\bullet\geq d
.
And similarly for
\overline{T_{\mathscr{G}^{(j)}}f}\supseteq M_{d}
.

iii.

The case of
T_{\mathscr{A}}f
is more delicate, see lemma
6.14
.

3.5.
Related derivations and related automorphisms. The “naturality” of vector fields integration

Given a map of (
C^{\infty}
or real/complex-analytic)
manifolds,
f:X\to{Y}
, take some vector fields
\xi_{X}\in\Theta_{X}
,
\xi_{Y}\in\Theta_{Y}
,
and the corresponding time-one flows, i.e. the automorphisms
\Phi_{X}\circlearrowright X
,

\Phi_{Y}\circlearrowright Y
.

Lemma 3.8
.

[Thom-Levine, e.g.
[
Mon. Nuñ.-Bal.
, Lemma 2.6]
] If the vector fields are related, i.e.
\xi_{Y}\circ f=df\circ\xi_{X}
, then so are the corresponding automorphisms,

\Phi_{Y}\circ f=f\circ\Phi_{X}
.

We need the algebraic version of this and other results on vector fields.
Below we use the exponential and logarithmic maps of §
2.5
.

Take a local filtered
\Bbbk
-algebra
(R_{X},{\mathfrak{m}},I^{\bullet}).
Assume
\Bbbk\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
and
R_{X}
is complete with respect to the filtration.
Take the filtration of
R^{\oplus p}_{X}
by
M_{\bullet}:=I^{\bullet}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
. Put
M_{\infty}:=0
. The map
exp
of (
9
) is well defined.

Lemma 3.9
.

Fix some elements
(\xi_{Y},\xi_{X})\in T_{\mathscr{A}^{(l)}},

f\in{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot R^{\oplus p}_{X}
, for some
l\geq 1,

1\leq d\leq\infty,
and

w_{d}\in M_{d}
.

1.

\xi_{Y}(e^{\xi_{X}}f)=e^{\xi_{X}}\cdot\xi_{Y}(y)|_{f}
.

2.

If
\xi_{X}(f)\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{\equiv}\limits^{mod\ M_{d}}}$}\ \xi_{Y}(y)|_{f}
then
\xi^{i}_{X}(\xi^{j}_{Y}(y)|_{f})\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{\equiv}\limits^{mod\ M_{d+l}}}$}\ \xi^{i+j}_{Y}(y)|_{f}
for any
i+j\geq 2
.

In particular,
e^{\xi_{X}}f-e^{\xi_{Y}}f\in M_{d}
.

3.

(algebraic “naturality”)
\xi_{Y}(y)|_{f}-\xi_{X}(f)\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{\equiv}\limits^{mod\ M_{d+l}}}$}\ w_{d}
if and only
if
e^{\xi_{Y}}f-e^{\xi_{X}}f\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{\equiv}\limits^{mod\ M_{d+l}}}$}\ w_{d}
.

Proof.

1.

The direct check:
\xi_{Y}(e^{\xi_{X}}f)=\xi_{Y}(y)|_{e^{\xi_{X}}f}=\xi_{Y}(y)|_{f\circ e^{\xi_{X}}}=e^{\xi_{X}}\cdot\xi_{Y}(y)|_{f}
.

2.

Induction on
i
. The case
i=0
is trivial. Assuming the statement for
i
we verify it for
i+1
:

(27)

\xi^{i+1}_{X}(\xi^{j}_{Y}(y)|_{f})=\xi_{X}\Big(\xi^{i}_{X}(\xi^{j}_{Y}(y)|_{f})\Big)=\xi_{X}\Big(\xi^{i+j}_{Y}(y)|_{f}\ mod\ M_{d}\Big)\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{\equiv}\limits^{mod\ M_{d+l}}}$}\ \xi_{X}\Big(\xi^{i+j}_{Y}(y)|_{f}\Big)=\\
\sum\nolimits_{q}\partial_{y_{q}}\Big(\xi^{i+j}_{Y}(y)\Big)|_{f}\cdot\xi_{X}(f_{q})\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{\equiv}\limits^{mod\ M_{d+l}}}$}\ \sum\nolimits_{q}\partial_{y_{q}}\Big(\xi^{i+j}_{Y}(y)\Big)|_{f}\cdot\xi_{Y}(y)_{q}|_{f}=\xi^{i+j+1}_{Y}(y)|_{f}.

The statement
e^{\xi_{X}}f-e^{\xi_{Y}}f\in M_{d}
is now obtained by Taylor expansion of the exponents.

3.

The part
"\Rrightarrow"
.

e^{\xi_{Y}}f-e^{\xi_{X}}f=w_{d}+\sum_{j\geq 2}\frac{\xi_{Y}^{j}(y)|_{f}-\xi_{X}^{j}(f)}{j!}\equiv w_{d}\ mod\ M_{d+l}
.
For the last equality we use part 2.

The part
"\Lleftarrow"
.

\xi_{Y}(y)|_{f}-\xi_{X}(f)=ln(e^{\xi_{Y}})(y)|_{f}-ln(e^{\xi_{X}})f\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{=}\limits^{!}}$}\ ln(e^{-\xi_{X}}e^{\xi_{Y}})(y)|_{f}=-\sum_{j\geq 1}\frac{(1-e^{-\xi_{X}}e^{\xi_{Y}})^{j}}{j}f=\sum_{j\geq 1}\frac{(1-e^{-\xi_{X}}e^{\xi_{Y}})^{j-1}}{j}e^{-\xi_{X}}(w_{d}\ mod\ M_{d+l})\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{\equiv}\limits^{mod\ M_{d+l}}}$}\ w_{d}
.
For the transition ”!” we use the commutativity
e^{\xi_{X}}e^{\xi_{Y}}=e^{\xi_{Y}}e^{\xi_{X}}
.

Remark 3.10
.

For the subsequent applications (§
4.3
, §
5.3
, §
7.4
) one needs the strongest Thom-Levine statement.
In Part 3 the conclusion cannot be strengthened to the statement “
(mod\ M_{d+l+1})
.”
For example, define the map
(\Bbbk^{1},o)\to(\Bbbk^{1},o)
by
f(x)=x+x^{d-1}\in\Bbbk[[x]]
. Here
\Bbbk\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
, the ring
R_{X}=\Bbbk[[x]]
is filtered by
(x)^{\bullet}
,
the ring
R_{Y}=\Bbbk[[y]]
is filtered by
(y)^{\bullet}
.

Take the derivations
\xi_{X}=x^{2}\partial_{x}
,
\xi_{Y}=y^{2}\partial_{y}
. One has
\xi_{X}(f)-\xi_{Y}(y)|_{f}\ \lower 1.72218pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\mathop{\equiv}\limits^{mod\ x^{2d-2}}}$}\ (d-3)x^{d}
.
But
e^{\xi_{X}}(x)=\frac{x}{1-x}
and therefore
e^{\xi_{X}}(f)-e^{\xi_{Y}}(f)=f(\frac{x}{1-x})-\frac{f(x)}{1-f(x)}=(d-3)x^{d}\big(1+\frac{3d-2}{2}x+\dots\big)
.
Thus
e^{\xi_{X}}(f)-e^{\xi_{Y}}(f)\not\equiv(d-3)x^{d}\ mod\ (x)^{d+2}
.

Remark 3.11
.

In the proof of part 3 we needed the exponential/logarithmic expansions only up to order
\lceil\frac{d-ord(f)}{l}\rceil+1
. Therefore this lemma can be
used even when the full exp/log maps do not exist, e.g. when
\Bbbk\not\supseteq\mathbb{Q}
or
R^{\oplus p}_{X}
is not
M_{\bullet}
